# List of world records in athletics



[Usain Bolt](4764846.md) beating [Tyson Gay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyson_Gay) and setting a [100 m world record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men's_100_metres_world_record_progression) at the [2009 World Championships in Athletics](3517978.md) in Berlin

[World records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_record) in [athletics](43719.md) are ratified by [World Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Athletics). Athletics records comprise the best performances in the sports of [track and field](194904.md), [road running](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_running) and [racewalking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racewalking).

Records are kept for [all events contested at the Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_at_the_Summer_Olympics) and some others. Unofficial records for some other events are kept by track and field statisticians. The only non-[metric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_system) track distance for which official records are kept is the [mile run](5227492.md).

## Criteria

The criteria which must be satisfied for ratification of a world record are defined by World Athletics in Part III of the Competition Rules.[^cite_note-Competition_Rules-1] These criteria also apply to national or other restricted records and also to performances submitted as qualifying marks for eligibility to compete in major events such as the [Olympic Games](27566.md).

The criteria include:



[David Rudisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Rudisha) en route to his 800 m world record at the [2012 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics)

- The dimensions of the track and equipment used must conform to standards. In road events, the course must be accurately measured, by a certified measurer.

- Except in road events ([road running](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_running) and [race walking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_walking)), the performance must be set in a single-sex race,

  [^cite_note-2]

  &#x20;with the sole exception of the mixed-sex 4 × 400 m relay, introduced by World Athletics in 2017.

  [^cite_note-3]

- All team members in a [relay race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relay_race) must be of the same [nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationality).

- [Pacemakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacemaker_\(running\)) are allowed, provided they have not been lapped; lapped athletes must give way.

- [Drug testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_testing) immediately after the performance is now required for ratification of a record. Existing records which predate this requirement are still extant. Athletes who pass the immediate test but are later found to have been using banned substances have their performances invalidated.

- In running events up to 200 m in distance and in horizontal jump events, [wind assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_assistance) is permitted only up to 2.0 [m/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metres_per_second). In [decathlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decathlon) or [heptathlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptathlon), average wind assistance of less than 2.0 m/s is required across all applicable disciplines; and maximum of 4.0 m/s in any one event. As an exception, according to rule 36.2, specific event organizers may choose to ignore wind velocity readings exclusively for their specific event records (e.g. a performance in a 100 m race at a meeting with a wind reading of +2.4 m/s may be considered that specific meeting record, but will not be considered as a world record).

  [^cite_note-Competition_Rules-1]

- In running events up to 800 m in distance, [photo finish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photo_finish) [fully automatic timing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fully_automatic_timing) is required.

- There is no restriction on altitude; since the thinner atmosphere of higher altitude provides less air resistance, locations such as [Mexico City](18987.md) and [Sestriere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sestriere) have previously been the sites of records in the sprint and jump events. See [effects of high altitude on humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_high_altitude_on_humans). Records set at high altitude venues are often marked with an "A" though that does not disqualify it as a record. Under those circumstances, a "sea level" best is also tracked by statisticians. Long-distance races run at altitude, with less oxygen available to the athlete, have been shown to be to the athlete's disadvantage.

- In road events, the course is not required to be a circuit, but the overall decrease in elevation between the start and finish shall not exceed 1\:1000, i.e. 1 m/km.

- In road events, the start and finish points of a course, measured along a theoretical straight line between them, shall not be further apart than 50% of the race distance.

See also: [Marathon world record progression § Criteria for record eligibility](310762.md#Criteria_for_record_eligibility)

## Bonus payments

Breaking a world record is an important event for athletics fans, and athletes' [personal sponsors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sponsor_\(commercial\)) and promoters of major meetings such as the [Diamond League](21778161.md) and its predecessor, the [IAAF Golden League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IAAF_Golden_League), have offered bonuses to athletes breaking a record.

Some [middle-distance runners](89280.md) have specialized in acting as [pacemakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacemaker_\(running\)) in longer races, receiving a fee without even finishing the race, and possibly a bonus if a record results. This is a useful occupation for athletes who are capable of running accurately to a specified pace, but not capable of the fastest times to become champions in their own right.

In the [pole vault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pole_vault) record bonuses create an incentive for an athlete capable of beating a record by a large margin to instead break it by the minimum amount (one [centimetre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centimetre)), multiple times, at multiple meetings, in order to accumulate multiple bonuses. This was done by [Sergey Bubka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Bubka) and [Yelena Isinbayeva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yelena_Isinbayeva) in the men's and women's pole vault respectively,[^cite_note-4] and some commentators have complained that neither athlete ever posted as high a mark as they were capable of.[^cite_note-5] Since 2020, [Armand Duplantis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_Duplantis) has been emulating the practice.[^cite_note-6] In most other disciplines, this issue does not arise, since it is practically impossible to deliberately break a record by a small margin.

## World records



[Sydney McLaughlin-Levrone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_McLaughlin-Levrone) being greeted with a "World records are my favorite food" sign at the [2022 World Athletics Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_World_Athletics_Championships)

World Athletics (then IAAF) commenced the recognition of world records in 1912, and indoor world records after 1987. In 2000, IAAF rule 260.18a (formerly 260.6a) was amended, so that "world records" (as opposed to "indoor world records") can be set in a facility "with or without roof". This rule was not applied retroactively,[^cite_note-7] and has, thus far, only affected the men's and women's [pole vault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pole_vault), women's 2000 m and women's [triple jump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_jump). The women's vault record has been advanced 9 times indoors by three different women, each ratified as a world record. The last record to be set indoors was in 2004. [Sergey Bubka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Bubka)'s 1993 pole vault world indoor record of 6.15 m was not considered to be a world record, because it was set before the new rule came into effect. Bubka's world record of 6.14 m, set outdoors in 1994, was surpassed by two different men since 2000, initially by [Renaud Lavillenie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaud_Lavillenie) in 2014 with a 6.16 m indoors mark, and most recently by [Armand Duplantis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_Duplantis) in 2026 with a 6.31 m indoors mark.

In 2023, World Athletics decided to introduce the new term 'short track' to replace the previous term 'indoor' to describe events and performances that are set on a 200 m track.[^cite_note-8] For track and combined events, the term "indoor world records" were changed to "short track world records". In some field events, including long jump, triple jump, high jump, pole vault, and shot put, indoor world records were eliminated. These changes came into effect on 1 November 2023.

### Key to tables

&#x20; *Awaiting ratification*   *not ratified or later rescinded by [World Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Athletics)*

h = hand timing \
&#x20;+ = [en route](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/en%20route) to a longer distance \
&#x20;A = affected by altitude \
&#x20;OT = oversized track \
&#x20;X = annulled due to doping violations \
&#x20;# = not officially ratified by [World Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Athletics) \
&#x20;a = aided road course according to IAAF rule 260.28 \
&#x20;i = set indoors \
&#x20;sh = short track, including "indoors" but also allowing for outdoor 200 metres tracks \
&#x20;italicised event names indicate those typically used in major championships

## World best performances

Records in events without World Athletics-ratified world records are typically referred to as world best performances or world bests.

While races over imperial measured distances were very common in the first half of the 20th century, only the [mile](5227492.md) remains common today due to its historical prominence in track and field: all other imperial measured distance races became increasingly rare, and the IAAF deleted these events from the world record books in 1976.

In November 2019, [World Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Athletics) (WA; formerly IAAF) also deleted several long-distance events (track distances of 20,000 metres, 25,000 metres and 30,000 metres[^cite_note-277] and road distances of 15 km, 20 km, 25 km and 30 km) from the world record books.

Some road racing distances and indoor variations of outdoor events fall outside of WA's lists, and records set in uncommon events usually do not adhere to the strict criteria found in WA-ratifiable events: one example is the 150 metres record, which was set by [Usain Bolt](4764846.md) on a specially-made straight track, while previous performances (such as the [Bailey–Johnson 150-metre race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bailey%E2%80%93Johnson_150-metre_race)) were completed on a traditional circuit which included a partial bend in the track.

The [40-yard dash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40-yard_dash), a standard acceleration evaluation for [American football](18951490.md) players, does not fall within the usual criteria of athletics racing events. In most 40-yard dashes, reaction times are not recorded as timing starts only once the player is in motion, and the standards for timing a "football 40" are so lax and inconsistent that a real world record cannot be claimed.

Performances are also hand-timed and calculated to 1/100 of a second, although studies have shown human beings simply cannot react consistently or accurately enough for this to be a valid method, and even those using light beams are timed by the motion of the athlete, removing the normal factor of reaction time; further, football 40-yard dashes are usually run on a turf surface as opposed to an [all weather track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_weather_track). All of these factors make track and "football 40" performances essentially impossible to compare.

The world best time for a "football 40" is 4.17 by [Deion Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deion_Sanders), while the extrapolated best for an Olympic-level athlete (including reacting to a starting gun) is 4.24 by [Maurice Greene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Greene_\(sprinter\)) at the [2001 World Championships in Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_World_Championships_in_Athletics).[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] Under conventional football timing on a turf field in 2017, [Christian Coleman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Coleman) reportedly ran a 4.12.[^cite_note-280]

### Combined event disciplines

World Athletics recognises world bests achieved in individual disciplines during a [combined event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_track_and_field_events).[^cite_note-l160-572] The below list includes disciplines in the [decathlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decathlon) (men) and [heptathlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptathlon) (women). Athletes must score at least 7000 points in a decathlon in order to have their performance recognised.[^cite_note-s068-573]

## Javelin specifications

See also: [Javelin redesign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javelin_redesign)

The men's javelin specification was changed with effect from 1986, and the women's from 2000. The purpose was to reduce the number of illegal flat landings, but a side-effect was to reduce the distance travelled. The prior world records in individual men's and women's javelin were invalidated, but the prior records in decathlon and heptathlon were not.[^cite_note-583]

The old specification records for men's and women's javelin were as follows:

| Event | Record | Athlete | Nat. | Pts | Date | Meeting | Location | Ctry. | Ref. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Men's | 104.80 m | [Uwe Hohn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uwe_Hohn) | [GDR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) | N/A | 20 Jul 1984 |  | [East Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Berlin) | [GDR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) | [^cite_note-584] |
| Women's | 80.00 m | [Petra Felke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra_Felke) | N/A | 9 Sep 1988 |  | [Potsdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potsdam) | [^cite_note-585] |  |  |

Retrieved 25 August 2024. When spectators watch him compete, they can see there is still a considerable amount of room between him and the bar when he jumps record heights. This begs the question, why doesn't Duplantis attempt the next world record height if he knows he can get it? The answer is money.
